[**Figure 4.2**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0002) Flow chart of Algorithm 4.1.

 

## 4.4 Numerical and Simulation Results

This section presents the numerical results and discussions. The simulation is completed using Matlab. First, evaluate the security of the user's information transmission in the presence of eavesdropping, by comparing the achieved secrecy rate using the proposed interference management mechanism against three alternative methods: complete interference elimination through non‐reused spectrum allocation without beamforming, interference management using only D2D communication without beamforming, and interference management using only spectrum reuse between MBS and SBSs without beamforming. The secrecy rate performance of a downlink HetNet cellular user is analyzed. In the simulation, the system model includes one MBS and two SBSs with spectrum reuse as the MBS. In the first tier, there are two users associated with the MBS. In each SBS, there is one user. D2D communications can be established at various distances from the eavesdropper's location. Assuming 10 antennas for the MBS and four antennas for each SBS, the MBS's transmit power is set to 10 W. The SINR threshold for all users is set to 0.6.

![Figure 4.3 Secrecy rate of the user under eavesdropping attack.](images/c04f003.png)

[**Figure 4.3**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0003) Secrecy rate of the user under eavesdropping attack.

The secrecy rate performance of the proposed method and three other interference management techniques are compared in [Figure 4.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0003). The label “Non” refers to total interference elimination through non‐reuse of spectrum without beamforming. “Only D2D” implies that only D2D interference is taken into account. “Only SR” denotes that only spectrum reuse between MBS and SBSs is considered. The secrecy rate is independent of the transmit power of SBSs for both the “Non” and “Only D2D” methods. In this simulation, the first tier is assumed to involve D2D communications, which may impact both the cellular user and the eavesdropper. [Figure 4.3](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0003) represents 5 D2D pairs. The label “The proposed” refers to the proposed methods for beamforming, spectrum reuse, and interference management in D2D communications. Without beamforming, D2D interference can improve the secrecy rate at a certain level. The use of spectrum reuse is the only method that can result in a decrease in secrecy rate as the transmission power of SBSs increases, without the use of beamforming. When beamforming is not employed, the use of spectrum reuse may lead to greater interference for the legitimate user compared to the eavesdropper.

![Figure 4.4 Secrecy rate with different number of D2D pairs.](images/c04f004.png)

[**Figure 4.4**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0004) Secrecy rate with different number of D2D pairs.

![Figure 4.5 Secrecy rate with different SINR requirements.](images/c04f005.png)

[**Figure 4.5**](#c04.xhtml#R_c04-fig-0005) Secrecy rate with different SINR requirements.

The secrecy rate of a user facing an eavesdropping attack was examined for different numbers of D2D pairs in [Figure 4.4](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0004). The simulation results indicate that the secrecy rate is maximized with four D2D pairs. This is because the presence of an additional D2D pair compared to three pairs can degrade the eavesdropper's communication more than the legitimate user. However, for five pairs of D2D users, one pair may degrade the legitimate user's communication more than the eavesdropper's. Therefore, not all the D2D interference in the network can be utilized to improve the secrecy rate. The proposed scheme's performance can be further improved by incorporating an iteration method into the D2D pair selection process.

[Figure 4.5](#c04.xhtml#c04-fig-0005) illustrates the secrecy rate performance considering different SINR requirements. For the simulation, all users were assigned the same SINR requirement. As higher SINR requirements limit the possibility of utilizing interference, the results show that a lower SINR requirement leads to a higher secrecy rate. In typical cellular communication systems, the SINR requirement is set to 0.6. When compared to a SINR requirement of 0.7, a 0.6 SINR requirement can exploit some interference to enhance the legitimate user's secrecy rate.

 

## 4.5 Conclusion

This chapter introduced an effective security solution for 5G wireless networks using the physical layer security approach. The proposed solution relies on interference management techniques. A security analysis was conducted for the proposed interference management mechanism for the downlink in a two‐tier HetNet, which includes beamforming, spectrum reuse, and D2D communications. In contrast to traditional interference management mechanisms that eliminate all interference, the proposed interference management mechanism can leverage beamforming and selective interference from spectrum reuse and D2D communications to enhance the security of communications in HetNet. Interference from SBSs to the user association with MBS can be strategically used to degrade the SINR of the eavesdropper while ensuring that the SINR requirement of other legitimate users is met. Additionally, D2D communications applied in the proximity of the eavesdropper can further reduce their SINR. However, it is important to note that not all interference in the network is beneficial for improving the secrecy rate.

# 5  
Flexible and Efficient Security Schemes for IoT Applications in 5G Wireless Systems

 

Recent advancements in communication technologies and integrated circuits have fueled the widespread adoption and integration of Internet‐of‐Things (IoT) applications, creating a pervasive environment rich in both challenges and opportunities. This chapter introduces a comprehensive system architecture for IoT applications leveraging 5G wireless networks. The architecture considers the heterogeneity of IoT devices, users, network operators, and service providers. Various trust models are proposed and evaluated based on the trust relationships among these entities. Building upon the previously discussed system architecture and trust model, a secure and flexible authentication and data transmission scheme is proposed for heterogeneous IoT devices using 5G wireless systems \[Fang et al., [2020](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0033)\].

 

## 5.1 IoT Application Models and Current Security Challenges

Ensuring security and privacy for IoT applications is a widely researched topic. However, given the diverse range of IoT application scenarios, creating a comprehensive system model for general IoT applications is challenging. Additionally, trust relationships between entities can vary depending on the specific application. In Kumar et al. [\[2017\]](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0048), an anonymous and secure framework for connected smart homes was proposed. The system model used was similar to that of wireless sensor networks, with the exception that the gateway is connected to the internet. However, previous studies have not adequately addressed the heterogeneity of IoT devices. Additionally, various scenarios for IoT device authentication have not been thoroughly investigated. In Song et al. [\[2017\]](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0084), only IoT devices and a server were considered in the system model for securing communications between these two parties. In Zhang et al. [\[2017a\]](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0114), the authors proposed a mobile‐health system model with three entities as network manager, wireless body area network clients, and medical service providers. Trust relationships between these entities were discussed in the proposed protocol. However, the heterogeneity of IoT devices is not considered in the system.

In addition to the IoT system model, IoT devices are vulnerable to various types of attacks such as physical, side‐channel, and cloning attacks due to their physical exposure and easy accessibility. Physical unclonable functions (PUFs) are considered to be built into IoT devices to provide security against those attacks. A PUF is a digital fingerprint that serves as a unique identity for a semiconductor device, which can derive a secret from the physical characteristics of the integrated circuits. Two primary applications of PUFs are strong authentication and cryptographic key generation \[Herder et al. [2014](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0042)\]. Considerable research has been devoted to assessing the properties of PUF hardware designs \[Maiti et al., [2012](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0058), Yan et al., [2017b](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0109), Gao et al., [2017](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0035), Cao et al., [2018b](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0014)\]. Furthermore, there have been efforts to develop authentication protocols using PUFs. For instance, \[Aman et al., [2017](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0008)\] proposed a mutual authentication and key exchange protocol based on PUFs. The protocol was designed to operate in two scenarios: between an IoT device and a server, and between two IoT devices. The proposed mutual authentication protocol relied on strong PUFs to achieve secure authentication. The protocol generated a session key using two random numbers, each generated by one of the participating entities. The research demonstrated that the protocol effectively guarded against cloning and physical attacks by utilizing PUFs. However, it should be noted that the protocol only considered IoT devices and servers.

Given the constraints of computational power and storage capacity of IoT devices, as well as privacy concerns, the current focus of research in IoT applications is on developing lightweight, efficient, and anonymous authentication methods. In Gope et al. [\[2018\]](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0038), the authors proposed a lightweight and privacy‐preserving authentication protocol for a radio frequency identification (RFID) system. The protocol was designed to operate in an ideal PUF environment and protect against various types of attacks, including DoS, eavesdropping, impersonation, and cloning attacks. However, these PUF‐based authentication methods are limited in their applicability to IoT systems as they only consider authentication between a tag and a server, and do not generate security keys post‐authentication. Several studies, such as Liu et al. [\[2014\]](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0054), Xiong [\[2014\]](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0101), Zhao [\[2014\]](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0116), and He et al. [\[2017\]](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0041), have proposed anonymous authentications. Although in \[Saeed et al., December 2018\] the authors proposed a cost‐effective and scalable authentication protocol, the previous works did not consider the comprehensive trust model and the heterogeneity of IoT devices. Therefore, this chapter aims to explore flexible and efficient security schemes for IoT applications that consider the comprehensive trust model and account for the heterogeneity of IoT devices.

 

## 5.2 A General System Model for IoT Applications Over 5G

This section presents a general IoT system architecture along with the corresponding trust models, threat models, and design objectives.

 

### 5.2.1 System Architecture

The architecture of the IoT system comprises four main components: heterogeneous IoT devices (classified as T1 and T2 IoT devices), IoT users, a network operator, and an IoT service provider. This is illustrated in [Figure 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0001), where the two types of heterogeneous IoT devices are differentiated based on their distinct capabilities.

![Figure 5.1 A general IoT system model over 5G networks.](images/c05f001.png)

[**Figure 5.1**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0001) A general IoT system model over 5G networks.

It is assumed that T1 IoT devices can directly connect to the network operator using either 3GPP or non‐3GPP access technologies, which allows them to communicate directly with the provider. T1 IoT devices offer superior communication and computational capabilities compared to T2 IoT devices, and come equipped with a built‐in security scheme. In contrast, T2 IoT devices are unable to establish a direct connection with the network operator. In addition, it is assumed that T2 IoT devices do not communicate with each other for the purposes of this study. Due to their limited battery life and computational capabilities, T2 IoT devices are unable to support complex security protocols. As such, a T1 IoT device is required to serve as a relay node for T2 IoT devices to connect to the network operator using non‐3GPP access technologies.

Secure communication channels must be established between all entities involved. As depicted in [Figure 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0001), the network operator is responsible for managing both the radio access networks and core networks. In 5G wireless systems, network slicing can be utilized to manage the resources based on specific applications dynamically. 5G core network differs from their 4G counterparts by segregating the control plane and data plane, which allows for improved performance and greater network flexibility during deployment \[Fang et al., [2017a](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0028), [2017b](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0029), [2018](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0030)\]. In the control plane, different network functions are allocated, such as the access and mobility management function (AMF), session management function (SMF), unified data management (UDM), authentication server function (AUSF), and policy control function (PCF) \[Fang et al., [2017a](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0028)\]. The programmable data plane makes core network more flexible and better support different use cases in 5G.

The IoT service provider provides data storage and possible data analysis services for IoT users. The IoT service provider can also manage the identities of IoT devices if needed. IoT users can communicate with IoT devices and the corresponding IoT service provider. The communications between IoT users and IoT devices can be wireless‐based or through the network operator. IoT users can communicate with the IoT service provider for data access and analysis services.

In the system model, there are assumed to be ![upper I](images/c05-i0001.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></math> T1 IoT devices, ![upper J](images/c05-i0002.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>J</mi></mrow></math> T2 IoT devices, ![upper N](images/c05-i0003.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>N</mi></mrow></math> IoT users, one IoT service provider, and one network operator. Typically, the value of ![upper J](images/c05-i0004.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>J</mi></mrow></math> is significantly greater than ![upper I](images/c05-i0005.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>I</mi></mrow></math>. A T1 IoT device ![upper D Subscript i Superscript 1](images/c05-i0006.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msubsup></math> can communicate with IoT users and the IoT service provider through the network operator. A T2 IoT device ![upper D Subscript j Superscript 2](images/c05-i0007.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>j</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></math> needs to go through a T1 IoT device to upload data to the IoT service provider and to communicate with other T1 IoT devices and IoT users. T1 IoT devices, IoT users, and the IoT service provider must first register with the network service provider before the communications between them. T2 IoT devices must first register with a T1 IoT device to join the system. Mutual authentication and initial session key agreement are required between two entities before they communicate with each other. T2 IoT devices must achieve mutual authentication with the IoT service provider to be able to connect to the network. Through a T1 IoT device, T2 IoT devices can join the network after the mutual authentication between T2 IoT devices and the IoT service provider. Moreover, all data transmission should be protected with data confidentiality, data integrity, and contextual privacy.

 

### 5.2.2 Trust Models

Trust models may vary depending on the specific applications in 5G wireless systems, which are used for a wide range of purposes. This study considers three potential trust models based on the IoT system model architecture presented in [Section 5.2.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0004), which takes into account the varying degrees of trust among the different entities discussed in the previous section. Given that T1 IoT devices, IoT users, and the IoT service provider communicate directly with the network operator, these entities can be collectively regarded as “network entities.” [Figure 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-fig-0002) illustrates a trust model for 5G networks based on the IoT system model.

![Figure 5.2 A trust model for 5G networks based on the IoT system model.](images/c05f002.png)

[**Figure 5.2**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-fig-0002) A trust model for 5G networks based on the IoT system model.

T2 IoT devices rely on T1 IoT devices to relay messages to other entities, but to maintain end‐to‐end security, T1 IoT devices must not be able to interpret messages exchanged between T2 IoT devices and other network entities. Various scenarios can be categorized according to the trust relationships among IoT users, T1 IoT devices, the network operator, and the IoT service provider, as outlined below.

*   Case 1: In this scenario, the network operator is deemed completely trustworthy by all other entities. Similarly, IoT users and IoT devices fully trust the IoT service provider. As such, the system model can treat the IoT service provider and network operator as a single entity. This configuration, known as Case 1, is commonly employed in early‐stage research.
*   Case 2: Both the network operator and the IoT service provider are semi‐trusted by IoT devices and IoT users. The network operator is fully trusted by the IoT service provider. The network operator and the IoT service provider are honest but curious about the data transmitted between IoT devices and IoT users. In this case, the IoT service provider can store information of IoT devices in the core network AUSF to achieve better latency management and mobility support. The system model can consider the IoT service provider and the network operator as one entity. Case 2 is also popularly used for simplifying the system model.
*   Case 3: Both the network operator and the IoT service provider are semi‐trusted by IoT devices and IoT users. The network operator and the IoT service provider are honest but curious about the data transmitted between IoT devices and IoT users. The IoT service provider only uses the network operator services but not trusts it to manage information of IoT devices. In this case, the IoT service provider can deploy some servers close to the IoT devices and users to shorten the communication latency. This case is not well studied but is more practical to real applications.

 

### 5.2.3 Threat Models and Design Objectives

Out of the available trust models, Case 3 model has been selected for this study due to its minimal trust requirements between entities. In light of the fact that T2 IoT devices are susceptible to various forms of attacks such as physical, side‐channel, and cloning attacks, it is assumed that these devices have weak PUFs. As a result, the IoT service provider only needs to store a single pair ![left-parenthesis upper C comma upper R right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0008.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>C</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>R</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> for each T2 device. We assume that eavesdropping attacks can be launched to get communication information between IoT devices and other network entities. An identity stolen attack can be launched by cloning the identities of IoT devices to connect to the network. Also consider that the network operator can capture authentication messages and service data of IoT devices, which can be used to reveal sensitive information about IoT users. The IoT service provider, offering various services to IoT devices and IoT users, has strong incentives to protect the service data to attract more IoT users to use their services. On the other hand, the IoT service provider is curious about the personal information of users for advertisement recommendations and sensitive information IoT users share with their collaborators. Consequently, it is assumed that the IoT service provider has the capability to disclose users' personal information to third‐party entities.

Building upon the Case 3 trust model and the threat model outlined earlier, the objective of this study is to develop a secure and adaptable authentication and data transmission scheme that ensures the safeguarding of privacy and security. The proposed scheme's design objectives are detailed in [Table 5.1](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0001).

[**Table 5.1**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0001)  Design objectives.

| Security services || Features |
| --- | --- |
| Mutual authentication || To ensure that each entity is who they claim to be, it is imperative that any two communicating entities within the system authenticate each other |
| --- | --- |
| Initial session key agreement || After successful authentication, it is essential that an initial session key is only shared between the two verified entities |
| Data confidentiality and integrity || When transmitting data between two entities, it is critical that the confidentiality of the data is maintained, ensuring that the privacy of the data is not compromised, and that the data remains unaltered throughout the transmission |
| Anonymity || It is ensured that the intended recipient is the only party that can access the sender's identity |
| Contextual privacy || It is ensured that any adversary is unable to link the source and destination of a message |
| Forward security || Even if an entity's private key is exposed, it will not compromise the transmission that was safeguarded by the preceding session key |
| End‐to‐end security || Only the two communicating entities can access the transmitted data, ensuring that it remains unreadable to any other party |
| Key escrow resilience || Any attempt by the network operator to impersonate other entities will be detected, preventing them from doing so |

 

## 5.3 The 5G Authentication and Secure Data Transmission Scheme

This section presents an investigation into an flexible and efficient scheme for authentication and secure data transmission, aiming to achieve mutual authentication and the agreement of an initial session key while maintaining anonymity. In addition to authentication, this scheme incorporates data confidentiality, data integrity protection, and contextual privacy in the transmitted data. The scheme also ensures forward security, end‐to‐end security, and key escrow resilience. Before delving into the specifics of the proposed scheme, an overview will be provided.

 

### 5.3.1 Overview of the 5G Authentication and Secure Data Transmission Scheme

To achieve the design goals while taking into account communication and computational efficiency, the proposed scheme offers distinct authentication methods for T2 IoT devices and other entities.

The system initialization is the first step, which requires T1 IoT devices, IoT users, and the IoT service provider to register with the network operator to generate their respective private and public keys. The second step is the authentication phase, which requires mutual authentication between the two communicating entities before any data transmission can take place. After successful authentication, the generation of an initial session key is necessary. The authentication of non‐T2 IoT devices differs from T2 IoT devices authentications. Before T2 IoT devices can communicate with any other entities, mutual authentication with a T1 IoT device and the IoT service provider is required. Mutual authentication between T2 IoT devices and the IoT service provider can be facilitated through the use of a T1 IoT device as a relay node. Depending on the application, the IoT service provider can simultaneously authenticate multiple T2 IoT devices. Once T2 IoT devices have joined the network, the relay T1 IoT device can assist in achieving mutual authentication between them and other entities. However, it is essential to establish an initial session key without disclosing it to the relay T1 IoT device.

Once authentication is complete, data transmission between these entities is facilitated with due consideration to data confidentiality, integrity protection, and contextual privacy. The proposed scheme also provides forward security, end‐to‐end security, and key escrow resilience, based on the trust model.

 

### 5.3.2 The Detailed Scheme

The flexible and efficient authentication and secure data transmission scheme includes five phases, system initialization, authentication and initial session key agreement, data transmission, data receiving, and T2 IoT devices authentication and initial session key agreement.

 

#### 5.3.2.1 Phase 1 – System Initialization

Phase 1 involves the initialization of the system, which comprises two primary steps – system parameter generation and registration.

1.  System parameter generation: The network operator generates two large prime numbers, ![p](images/c05-i0009.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow></math> and ![q](images/c05-i0010.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow></math>, based on a security parameter ![k](images/c05-i0011.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></math> that determines the input size of the computational problem. Let ![upper P](images/c05-i0012.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>P</mi></mrow></math> be a generator of a cyclic group ![upper G](images/c05-i0013.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>G</mi></mrow></math> with order ![q](images/c05-i0014.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow></math> on elliptic‐curve cryptography (ECC) \[Stallings, [2017](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0086)\]. The network operator randomly generates ![x Subscript n Baseline element-of upper Z Subscript q Superscript asterisk](images/c05-i0015.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>&#8712;</mo><msubsup><mi>Z</mi><mi>q</mi><mo>&#42;</mo></msubsup></math> as its own master private key and computes the public key ![upper X Subscript n Baseline equals x Subscript n Baseline upper P](images/c05-i0016.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mi>P</mi></math>. Additionally, the network operator chooses six secure hash functions: ![upper H 0 colon StartSet 0 comma 1 EndSet Superscript asterisk Baseline times upper Z Subscript q Superscript asterisk Baseline 2.  Registration: The registrations process for joining the system involves two distinct stages. During the first stage, T1 IoT devices, IoT users, and the IoT service provider register directly with the network operator. In the second stage, T2 IoT devices register with their corresponding relay T1 IoT device to gain access to the system.

For the network operator, T1 IoT devices (![upper D Superscript 1](images/c05-i0024.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>D</mi><mn>1</mn></msup></math>), IoT users (![upper U](images/c05-i0025.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>U</mi></mrow></math>), and the IoT service provider (![upper S](images/c05-i0026.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>S</mi></mrow></math>) are network entities. Let ![upper W](images/c05-i0027.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> be a network entity, where ![upper W element-of StartSet upper D Superscript 1 Baseline comma upper U comma upper S EndSet](images/c05-i0028.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>W</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#123;</mo><msup><mi>D</mi><mn>1</mn></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>U</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>S</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#125;</mo></math>. The registration steps for W are as follows.

*   ![upper W](images/c05-i0029.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> randomly selects ![x Subscript w Baseline element-of upper Z Subscript q Superscript asterisk](images/c05-i0030.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>&#8712;</mo><msubsup><mi>Z</mi><mi>q</mi><mo>&#42;</mo></msubsup></math> as a partial secret value and computes ![upper X Subscript w Baseline equals x Subscript w Baseline upper P](images/c05-i0031.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mi>P</mi></math> as its corresponding partial public key.
*   ![upper W](images/c05-i0032.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> sends its identity and the partial public key ![left-parenthesis upper W comma upper X Subscript w Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0033.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>W</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> to the network operator for registration.
*   The network operator randomly chooses ![y Subscript w Baseline element-of upper Z Subscript q Superscript asterisk](images/c05-i0034.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>&#8712;</mo><msubsup><mi>Z</mi><mi>q</mi><mo>&#42;</mo></msubsup></math> and computes ![upper Y Subscript w Baseline equals y Subscript w Baseline upper P](images/c05-i0035.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mi>P</mi></math> and ![z Subscript w Baseline equals y Subscript w Baseline plus x Subscript n Baseline upper H 1 left-parenthesis upper W StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper X Subscript w Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper Y Subscript w Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper X Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0036.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>z</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>W</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> to register ![upper W](images/c05-i0037.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> with the partial public key ![upper X Subscript w](images/c05-i0038.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub></math>.
*   The network operator sends the partial private key ![z Subscript w](images/c05-i0039.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>z</mi><mi>w</mi></msub></math> and partial public key ![upper Y Subscript w](images/c05-i0040.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub></math> through secure channel to ![upper W](images/c05-i0041.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> and stores the public key ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript w Baseline comma upper Y Subscript w Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0042.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

Once ![upper W](images/c05-i0043.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> receives ![left-parenthesis z Subscript w Baseline comma upper Y Subscript w Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0044.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, it can verify the validity of these two keys by comparing ![z Subscript w Baseline upper P](images/c05-i0045.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>z</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mi>P</mi></math> with ![upper Y Subscript w Baseline plus upper X Subscript n Baseline upper H 1 left-parenthesis upper W StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper X Subscript w Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper Y Subscript w Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper X Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0046.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>W</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. Then ![upper W](images/c05-i0047.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> stores the full private key ![left-parenthesis x Subscript w Baseline comma z Subscript w Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0048.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and the public key ![left-parenthesis upper X Subscript w Baseline comma upper Y Subscript w Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0049.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

For T2 IoT devices to join the system, they must register at a relay T1 IoT device. Assume the identity of ![i th](images/c05-i0050.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>i</mi><mtext>th</mtext></math> T2 IoT device is ![upper D Subscript i Superscript 2](images/c05-i0051.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></math>. ![upper R](images/c05-i0052.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>R</mi></mrow></math> represents the relay T1 IoT device. Then the relay T1 IoT device computes ![l Subscript i Baseline equals upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper D Subscript i Superscript 2 Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue x Subscript r Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0053.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>r</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and ![upper P upper D Subscript i Superscript 2 Baseline equals upper H 5 left-parenthesis upper X Subscript r Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper Y Subscript r Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue l Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0054.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>r</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><mi>r</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. Both ![l Subscript i](images/c05-i0055.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></math> and ![upper P upper D Subscript i Superscript 2](images/c05-i0056.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></math> will be programmed into the T2 IoT device before deploying it into the network.

The authentication between T2 IoT devices and the IoT service provider is discussed in phase 5.

 

#### 5.3.2.3 Phase 3 – Data Transmission

Data transmission in the system involves two types: one is between network entities, and the other is between T2 IoT devices and a single network entity.

1.  Data transmission between network entities: To transmit a message ![m](images/c05-i0098.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> from network entity ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0099.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> to network entity ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0100.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>, the message must be routed through the network operator. Both network entities will first find the session key of the current session ![t](images/c05-i0101.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow></math> as ![k Subscript t Superscript w 1 comma w 2](images/c05-i0102.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mi>t</mi><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup></math>. Subsequently, the sender entity, ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0103.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math>, will undertake the following steps.
    
    *   The sender ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0104.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> randomly chooses ![g element-of upper Z Subscript q Superscript asterisk](images/c05-i0105.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>g</mi><mo>&#8712;</mo><msubsup><mi>Z</mi><mi>q</mi><mo>&#42;</mo></msubsup></math> and computes ![h 1 equals upper H 1 left-parenthesis upper W 2 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper X Subscript w 2 Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper Y Subscript w 2 Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper X Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0106.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>h</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, ![v 1 equals g upper X Subscript w 2](images/c05-i0107.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>g</mi><msub><mi>X</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub></math>;
    *   To ensure the data source identity and integrity, ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0108.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> computes ![s 1 equals g upper P comma s 2 equals upper H 4 left-parenthesis s 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper W 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue m right-parenthesis plus upper H 4 left-parenthesis v 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper W 2 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue m right-parenthesis comma s 3 equals g slash left-parenthesis x Subscript w 1 Baseline plus z Subscript w 1 Baseline plus s 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0109.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>g</mi><mi>P</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>m</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>g</mi><mo stretchy="false">/</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>;
    *   To ensure the data confidentiality, ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0110.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> computes ![v 2 equals g left-parenthesis upper Y Subscript w 2 Baseline plus h 1 upper X Subscript n Baseline right-parenthesis comma m prime equals upper H 5 left-parenthesis v 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue v 2 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue k Subscript t Superscript w 1 comma w 2 Baseline right-parenthesis circled-plus m](images/c05-i0111.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mi>g</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>h</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>m</mi><mo>&#8242;</mo></msup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mi>t</mi><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8853;</mo><mi>m</mi></math>;

    ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0113.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> sends the data as ![left-parenthesis upper E Subscript upper X Sub Subscript w 2 Subscript Baseline left-parenthesis upper W 1 right-parenthesis comma left-parenthesis s 1 comma s 2 comma s 3 comma m prime comma t right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0114.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><msub><mi>X</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>m</mi><mo>&#8242;</mo></msup><mo>,</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> through the network operator, and refreshes the session key between ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0115.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> as ![k Subscript t plus 1 Superscript w 1 comma w 2 Baseline equals upper H 0 left-parenthesis t StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue k Subscript t Superscript w 1 comma w 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0116.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mi>t</mi><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

If ![upper H 4 left-parenthesis s 3 left-parenthesis upper X Subscript w 1 Baseline plus upper Y Subscript w 1 Baseline plus s 2 upper P right-parenthesis StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper W 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue m double-prime right-parenthesis plus upper H 4 left-parenthesis v prime 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper W 2 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue m double-prime right-parenthesis equals s 2](images/c05-i0129.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>Y</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msup><mi>m</mi><mo>&#8243;</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>v</mi><mn>1</mn><mo>&#8242;</mo></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msup><mi>m</mi><mo>&#8243;</mo></msup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>s</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>, the message ![m double-prime](images/c05-i0130.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>m</mi><mo>&#8243;</mo></msup></math> is accepted.

![upper W 2](images/c05-i0131.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> refreshes the session key between ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0132.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> as ![k Subscript t plus 1 Superscript w 1 comma w 2 Baseline equals upper H 0 left-parenthesis t StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue k Subscript t Superscript w 1 comma w 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0133.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mrow><mi>t</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>t</mi><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mi>t</mi><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

 

#### 5.3.2.5 Phase 5 – T2 IoT Devices Authentication and Initial Session Key Agreement

T2 IoT devices must pass three levels of authentication. Firstly, they must establish mutual authentication with the relay T1 IoT device to enable a connection after registration. Secondly, through the T1 IoT device, T2 IoT devices must achieve mutual authentication with the IoT service provider to be granted system access. This authentication uses a PUF function to verify the legitimacy of both the T2 IoT device and the IoT service provider. Thirdly, once connected to the system, T2 IoT devices must undergo mutual authentication with other network entities. The relay T1 IoT device facilitates the second and third levels of authentication.

For the authentication between a T2 IoT device and the relay T1 IoT device, the relay T1 IoT device sends its public key ![upper X Subscript r](images/c05-i0134.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>r</mi></msub></math> to the T2 IoT device. The ![i th](images/c05-i0135.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>i</mi><mtext>th</mtext></math> T2 IoT device randomly generates ![upper N 0](images/c05-i0136.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math> and sends ![left-parenthesis upper P upper D Subscript i Superscript 2 Baseline comma upper N 0 comma upper E Subscript upper X Sub Subscript r Subscript Baseline left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue l Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0137.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>P</mi><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>r</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> to the relay T1 IoT device. The relay T1 IoT device decrypts and verifies the message ![upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue l Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0138.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, and randomly generates ![upper N 1](images/c05-i0139.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> and sends ![left-parenthesis upper N 0 circled-plus upper N 1 comma upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue l Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis comma right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0140.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>&#8853;</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. The T2 IoT device finds out ![upper N 1](images/c05-i0141.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> and verifies the ![upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue l Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0142.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. The T2 IoT device and the relay T1 IoT device establish an initial session key by computing ![k 0 Superscript upper D Super Subscript i Super Superscript 2 Superscript comma r Baseline equals upper H 0 left-parenthesis left-parenthesis upper N 0 circled-plus upper N 1 right-parenthesis StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue l Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0143.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>&#8853;</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, which can be used to secure the communications between them.

After the mutual authentication between a T2 IoT device and the relay T1 IoT device, mutual authentication between the T2 IoT device and the IoT service provider is needed to allow the T2 IoT device to join the network. Through the relay node, the IoT service provider can authenticate the T2 IoT device through the following steps.

*   The relay T1 IoT device sends message ![m 1 equals left-parenthesis upper D 1 squared comma upper D 2 squared comma ellipsis comma upper D Subscript upper I Superscript 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0144.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>m</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mn>2</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>I</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> to the IoT service provider, where ![upper D Subscript i Superscript 2](images/c05-i0145.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></math> is the ![i th](images/c05-i0146.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>i</mi><mtext>th</mtext></math> T2 IoT device connecting to the relay T1 IoT device.
*   After the challenge message is sent by the T2 IoT device, the IoT service provider authenticates the message by verifying it with the corresponding device identity ![upper C equals left-parenthesis left-parenthesis upper C 1 comma upper D 1 squared right-parenthesis comma left-parenthesis upper C 2 comma upper D 2 squared right-parenthesis comma ellipsis comma left-parenthesis upper C Subscript upper I Baseline comma upper D Subscript upper I Superscript 2 Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0147.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>C</mi><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mn>1</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mn>2</mn><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>&#8230;</mi><mo>,</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>I</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>I</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. Upon successful verification, the IoT service provider sends a nonce ![upper N 0](images/c05-i0148.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math> along with the verified message to the relay T1 IoT device.
*   The relay T1 IoT device sends each T2 IoT device the corresponding challenge ![left-parenthesis upper C Subscript i Baseline comma upper E Subscript k 0 Sub Superscript upper D Sub Super Subscript i Sub Super Superscript 2 Sub Superscript comma r Subscript Baseline left-parenthesis upper N 0 right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0149.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.
*   Each T2 IoT device computes a hash value of ![upper E Subscript k 0 Sub Superscript upper D Sub Super Subscript i Sub Super Superscript 2 Sub Superscript comma r Baseline left-parenthesis upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue f left-parenthesis upper C Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0150.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>E</mi><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and sends it to the relay T1 IoT device, where ![f left-parenthesis upper C Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis element-of upper Z Subscript q Superscript asterisk](images/c05-i0151.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8712;</mo><msubsup><mi>Z</mi><mi>q</mi><mo>&#42;</mo></msubsup></math>.
*   The relay T1 IoT device decrypts the message, and sends ![m 2 equals left-parenthesis upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue f left-parenthesis upper C 1 right-parenthesis right-parenthesis circled-plus upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue f left-parenthesis upper C 2 right-parenthesis right-parenthesis circled-plus midline-horizontal-ellipsis circled-plus upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue f left-parenthesis upper C Subscript upper I Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0152.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>m</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8853;</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8853;</mo><mi>&#8943;</mi><mo>&#8853;</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>I</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.
*   The IoT service provider verifies the message, sends the confirmation to the relay T1 IoT device, and generates a secret key between itself and the ![i th](images/c05-i0153.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>i</mi><mtext>th</mtext></math> T2 IoT device ![k Superscript upper D Super Subscript i Super Superscript 2 Superscript comma s Baseline equals upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper D Subscript i Superscript 2 Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue f left-parenthesis upper C Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0154.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>k</mi><mrow><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

Both the relay T1 IoT device and each T2 IoT device refresh their session key ![upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue k 0 Superscript upper D Super Subscript i Super Superscript 2 Superscript comma r Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0155.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>r</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.
*   The ![i th](images/c05-i0156.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>i</mi><mtext>th</mtext></math> T2 IoT device generates the session key between itself and the IoT service provider ![k Superscript upper D Super Subscript i Super Superscript 2 Superscript comma s Baseline equals upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper D Subscript i Superscript 2 Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper N 0 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue f left-parenthesis upper C Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0157.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>k</mi><mrow><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow></msup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>C</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

After T2 IoT devices join the network, other network entities can communicate with T2 IoT devices through the relay T1 IoT device. The authentication process consists of the following steps.

*   ![upper W](images/c05-i0158.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> initiates a communication request by sending a nonce ![upper N 0](images/c05-i0159.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math> and the pseudonym ![upper P upper D Subscript i Superscript 2](images/c05-i0160.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mi>P</mi><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup></math> of the T2 IoT device to the relay T1 IoT device.
*   The relay T1 IoT device verifies the network entity and sends public key ![upper X Subscript w](images/c05-i0161.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub></math> and ![upper N 0](images/c05-i0162.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></math> of the network entity ![upper W](images/c05-i0163.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math> to the T2 IoT device.
*   The T2 IoT device randomly generates ![upper N 1](images/c05-i0164.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> and computes ![upper E Subscript upper X Sub Subscript w Baseline left-parenthesis upper N 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0165.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>E</mi><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and ![upper H 0 left-parenthesis l Subscript i Baseline StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper E Subscript upper X Sub Subscript w Subscript Baseline left-parenthesis upper N 1 right-parenthesis right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0166.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>, which are sent to the relay T1 IoT device. The T2 IoT device generates a session key ![k 0 Superscript upper D Super Subscript i Super Superscript 2 Superscript comma w Baseline equals upper H 0 left-parenthesis left-parenthesis upper N 0 circled-plus upper N 1 right-parenthesis StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper X Subscript w Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0167.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>&#8853;</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.
*   The relay T1 IoT device verifies ![upper H 0 left-parenthesis upper E Subscript upper X Sub Subscript w Subscript Baseline left-parenthesis upper N 1 right-parenthesis StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue l Subscript i Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0168.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>E</mi><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>l</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and sends ![upper E Subscript upper X Sub Subscript w Baseline left-parenthesis upper N 1 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0169.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>E</mi><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> to the network entity ![upper W](images/c05-i0170.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow></math>.
*   The network entity decrypts the message with its full private key and generates the session key ![k 0 Superscript upper D Super Subscript i Super Superscript 2 Superscript comma w Baseline equals upper H 0 left-parenthesis left-parenthesis upper N 0 circled-plus upper N 1 right-parenthesis StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue upper X Subscript w Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0171.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msubsup><mi>D</mi><mi>i</mi><mn>2</mn></msubsup><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>&#8853;</mo><msub><mi>N</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>X</mi><mi>w</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>.

 

## 5.4 Security Analysis

This section provides an analysis on the security properties of the proposed scheme. Specifically, it evaluates the proposed protocol's ability to meet the design objectives in [Section 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-sec-0003).

 

### 5.4.1 Protocol Verification

The first step is to verify the protocol for both the authentication and key setup procedures. Upon receiving the message ![left-parenthesis upper Q 1 comma upper Q 2 right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0172.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>Q</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>Q</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> from ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0173.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math>, ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0174.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> first needs to decrypt the ciphertext using its full private key ![left-parenthesis x Subscript w 2 Baseline comma z Subscript w 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0175.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> as follows.

### 5.4.2 Security Objectives

This section verifies the achieved security objectives based on the proposed protocol.

 

#### 5.4.2.1 Mutual Authentication

The proposed scheme can achieve mutual authentication for multiple scenarios, including situations where sender ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0192.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> needs to authenticate itself to receiver ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0193.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>. To achieve this, ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0194.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> generates a hash value using both its own private key and the full private key of ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0195.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>, which ensures that only ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0196.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> can authenticate the message. Specifically, the proposed scheme ensures that only the receiver is able to recover the identity of the sender and authenticate itself to the sender using the same approach. The mutual authentication between a T2 IoT device and other entities is achieved through the help of a relay T1 IoT device. To join the system, the T2 IoT device must first undergo mutual authentication with the relay T1 IoT device. This is achieved using a pre‐shared hash value that is known to both devices. Once authenticated, the T2 IoT device can proceed to authenticate itself to the IoT service provider. This is accomplished using a PUF function, with assistance from the relay T1 IoT device. Finally, to authenticate itself to other network entities, the T2 IoT device relies on the relay T1 IoT device.

 

#### 5.4.2.2 Initial Session Key Agreement

After successful authentication, the two network entities ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0197.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> and ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0198.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> are able to share a secure session key. The key materials ![a](images/c05-i0199.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow></math> and ![b](images/c05-i0200.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow></math> are unique to the two network entities, meaning that they can only be shared between them. In other words, these key materials can only be shared by these two network entities. More specifically, ![k 0 Superscript w 1 comma w 2](images/c05-i0201.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup></math> can only be computed by the network entity ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0202.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> with its full private key ![left-parenthesis x Subscript w 2 Baseline comma z Subscript w 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0203.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and ![b](images/c05-i0204.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow></math> which is generated by ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0205.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>. And ![k 0 Superscript w 1 comma w 2](images/c05-i0206.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mn>0</mn><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup></math> can only be computed by the network entity ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0207.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> with its full private key ![left-parenthesis x Subscript w 1 Baseline comma z Subscript w 1 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0208.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math> and ![a](images/c05-i0209.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow></math> which is generated by ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0210.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math>. Using the key materials exchanged during the authentication phase, the proposed scheme can ensure that the two parties have correctly shared the session key.

 

#### 5.4.2.3 Data Confidentiality and Integrity

The proposed scheme ensures data confidentiality and integrity through the use of several measures. Specifically, data confidentiality is protected using both a session key ![k Subscript t Superscript w 1 comma w 2](images/c05-i0211.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mi>t</mi><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup></math> and the public key encryption. The sender ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0212.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> encrypts the data using the public key of the receiver and the session key that is shared between the sender ![upper W 1](images/c05-i0213.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></math> and the receiver ![upper W 2](images/c05-i0214.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>W</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>. The message ![m](images/c05-i0215.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mrow><mi>m</mi></mrow></math> is encrypted to ![m prime equals upper H 5 left-parenthesis v 1 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue v 2 StartAbsoluteValue EndAbsoluteValue k Subscript t Superscript w 1 comma w 2 Baseline right-parenthesis circled-plus m](images/c05-i0216.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msup><mi>m</mi><mo>&#8242;</mo></msup><mo>=</mo><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><mo stretchy="false">&#124;</mo><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mi>t</mi><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>&#8853;</mo><mi>m</mi></math>, where ![v 1 comma v 2](images/c05-i0217.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> only can be recovered with the receiver's full private key ![left-parenthesis x Subscript w 2 Baseline comma z Subscript w 2 Baseline right-parenthesis](images/c05-i0218.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></math>. The session key ![k Subscript t Superscript w 1 comma w 2](images/c05-i0219.png)<math display="inline" overflow="scroll"><msubsup><mi>k</mi><mi>t</mi><mrow><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>w</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow></msubsup></math> is only shared and stored between the sender and the receiver.

Data integrity is guaranteed with the signature of the sender. By decrypting and verifying the ciphertext ![left-parenthesis upper E Subscript upper X Sub Subscript w 1 Subscript Baseline left-parenthesis upper W 1 right-parenthesis comma left-parenthesis s 1 integrity of the message.

 

#### 5.4.2.4 Contextual Privacy

The proposed scheme achieves contextual privacy by ensuring that the source identity of a message is not transmitted in plaintext. Rather, only the intended receiver, using its private key, can retrieve the source identity of the message.

 

#### 5.4.2.5 Forward Security

After the initial session key generation, a new session key is generated at the end of each transmission, which helps to ensure forward secrecy. To generate the new session key, a hash function is applied to the current session key. Due to the one‐way property of the hash function, attackers cannot recover the previous session key even if they get the current session key. Moreover, even if the full private key of the network entity is revealed, the previous transmission remains confidential since the previous session key is not able to be recovered.

 

#### 5.4.2.6 End‐to‐End Security

The proposed scheme ensures end‐to‐end security, meaning that messages exchanged between any two entities are not accessible to the network operator as they lack a partial private key generated by the user. The full private key and the session key are required to decrypt messages, and only the receiver possesses both. Additionally, the session key is exclusively known by the communicating entities, ensuring that end‐to‐end security is maintained.

 

#### 5.4.2.7 Key Escrow Resilience

The proposed scheme features a private key generation process that involves both the network operator and the network entity. Each entity's private key consists of a partial key generated by the entity itself, and another partial key generated by the network operator. This dual‐generation approach ensures that the network operator cannot impersonate any other entity undetected, as the entity‐generated partial key acts as a safeguard against such malicious attempts.

 

## 5.5 Performance Evaluation

This section evaluates the performance of the proposed scheme in three key areas: security services provided, computational overhead, and communication overhead. By analyzing these metrics, the effectiveness of the scheme can be accessed in providing robust security while minimizing the computational and communication costs associated with its implementation.

 

### 5.5.1 Security Services

This section compares the security services provided by the proposed scheme with those of other similar schemes. It's worth noting that most existing authentication schemes are limited to a single scenario involving one type of IoT device and a server, due to their simplified system models. However, the proposed scheme overcomes this limitation by providing a comprehensive set of security services that are adaptable to a wide range of scenarios and devices within the IoT ecosystem. The proposed mutual authentication scheme is highly versatile and can be adapted to suit a variety of scenarios, thanks to the proposed system model architecture. Additionally, the scheme goes beyond simple authentication to provide a range of robust security services, including confidentiality and integrity, and contextual privacy of data transmission. These measures help ensure the privacy of sensitive information during transmission. Further details of the security services comparison can be found in [Table 5.2](#c05.xhtml#c05-tbl-0002), where 1 = \[Saeed et al., [2018](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0074)\], 2 = \[Liu et al., [2014](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0054)\], 3 = \[Xiong, [2014](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0101)\], 4 = \[Zhao, [2014](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0116)\], 5 = \[Wang and Zhang, [2015](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0093)\], and 6 = \[He et al., [2017](#b01.xhtml#bref-bib-0041)\].

[**Table 5.2**](#c05.xhtml#R_c05-tbl-0002)  Security services comparison.

| Schemes || Mutual authentication || Contextual privacy || Data confidentiality || Forward security || End‐to‐end security || Key escrow resilience |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 || Yes (single scenario) || No || Yes || Yes || Yes || Yes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2 || Yes (single scenario) || No || Yes || No || Yes || Yes |
| 3 || Yes (single scenario) || No || Yes || Yes || Yes || Yes |
| 4 || Yes (single scenario) || No || Yes || No || Yes || Yes |
| 5 || Yes (single scenario) || No || Yes || Yes || Yes || Yes |
| 6 || Yes (single scenario) || No || Yes || Yes || Yes || Yes |
| The proposed || Yes (multiple scenario) || Yes || Yes || Yes || Yes || Yes |
